</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="Abs1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="Par1">Alzheimer’s Disease (AD) is a very common neurodegenerative disorders and early detection using electroencephalography (EEG) can enable timely intervention, however, existing computational models often lack robustness, interpretability, and clinical scalability. This study proposes NeuroFusionNet, a hybrid deep learning framework for accurate, explainable, and efficient EEG-based classification of Alzheimer’s Disease and related dementias. The model fuses handcrafted spectral, statistical, wavelet, and entropy features with latent temporal embeddings extracted from a customized one-dimensional convolutional neural network (1D-CNN). Feature selection is performed using Pearson Correlation Coefficient (PCC) and Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO), Principal Component Analysis (PCA)-based dimensionality reduction, and SMOTE-based class balancing has been performed to enhance discriminative learning. Comprehensive preprocessing including bandpass filtering, Artifact Subspace Reconstruction (ASR), and Independent Component Analysis (ICA) improves signal quality prior to classification through a five-layer deep neural network optimized via adaptive learning rate scheduling. Proposed method has been validated on three public EEG datasets including OpenNeuro ds004504 (eyes-closed), ds006036 (eyes-open), and the independent OSF dataset. Our method demonstrates state-of-the-art accuracy and macro F-1 score of 94.27% and 0.94 respectively. Cross-validation yielded minimal variance (SD &lt;0.3%) that confirms the robustness and reproducibility. Model interpretability was ensured using Shapley Additive Explanations (SHAP) and Gradient-weighted Class Activation Mapping (Grad-CAM), which revealed physiologically consistent biomarkers such as posterior alpha attenuation and frontal–theta enhancement patterns well aligned with established AD pathophysiology. Demographic fairness analysis showed negligible bias (&lt;0.6% difference) across gender and age subgroups. Despite its high accuracy, NeuroFusionNet remains lightweight (0.94M parameters, 4.1 MB footprint) and computationally efficient (6.5 ms inference per sample), enabling real-time deployment on standard clinical CPUs without GPU support. </p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Subject terms:</strong> Computational biology and bioinformatics, Engineering, Mathematics and computing, Neurology, Neuroscience</p></section></section><section id="Sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p id="Par2">Alzheimer’s Disease (AD) is a degenerative neurological disorder which is caused by progressive cognitive decline, synaptic dysfunction, and neuronal loss. It is considered as incurable and one of the leading causes of dementia worldwide. More than 50 million individuals currently suffer from AD globally, with estimated increase to nearly 152 million by 2050<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>–<a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a></sup>. AD typically progresses through three clinical stages mild, moderate, and severe as summarized in Table <a href="#Tab1" class="usa-link">1</a>. Despite extensive research, available pharmacological treatments approved by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) primarily target symptomatic management rather than treatment of the disease<sup><a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a>,<a href="#CR5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR5">5</a></sup>. Therefore, early detection of Mild Cognitive Impairment (MCI) or prodromal stage is essential for timely intervention before neurological damage occurs<sup><a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>–<a href="#CR8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR8">8</a></sup>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="Tab1"><h3 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h3>
<div class="caption p"><p>Progression of Alzheimer’s Disease: Neuropathological, Cognitive, and Functional Characteristics.</p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="hsides" rules="groups">
<thead><tr>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Stage</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Neuropathological features</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Cognitive / behavioral symptoms</th>
<th align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Functional status</th>
</tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Mild AD</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Presence of Amyloid Plaques and Tau Tangles mainly in hippocampal and cortical regions.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq1"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcd0eadbe23f/d33e292.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e292" alt="Inline graphic"></span> Memory loss</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq2"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcd0eadbe23f/d33e297.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e297" alt="Inline graphic"></span> Confusion</p>
</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Independent in most daily activities; noticeable forgetfulness and reduced recall.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Moderate AD</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Increased spread of plaques and tangles into temporal and parietal lobes.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq3"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcd0eadbe23f/d33e310.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e310" alt="Inline graphic"></span> Poor judgment</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq4"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcd0eadbe23f/d33e315.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e315" alt="Inline graphic"></span> Wandering</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq5"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcd0eadbe23f/d33e320.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e320" alt="Inline graphic"></span> Agitation</p>
</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Requires assistance with daily tasks; changes in personality and behavior become evident.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Severe AD</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Widespread neuronal loss and cortical atrophy; dense plaques and tangles across multiple brain regions.</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq6"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcd0eadbe23f/d33e333.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e333" alt="Inline graphic"></span> Loss of speech</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq7"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcd0eadbe23f/d33e338.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e338" alt="Inline graphic"></span> Incontinence</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq8"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcd0eadbe23f/d33e343.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e343" alt="Inline graphic"></span> Bedridden</p>
</td>
<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">Completely dependent on caregivers; loss of voluntary motor control and cognitive functions.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par3">These progressive neuropathological changes underscore the urgent need for early and accurate diagnostic frameworks that can identify subtle cognitive and neural alterations well before severe cortical damage occurs.</p>
<p id="Par4">To address this challenge, researchers have proposed electroencephalography (EEG) a non-invasive, cost-effective, and temporally precise technique capable of capturing subtle neural dynamics associated with cognitive impairment<sup><a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">9</a>–<a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">11</a></sup>. EEG signals reflect the brain’s oscillatory activity across multiple frequency bands, each corresponding to distinct cognitive and awareness states. Figure <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a> summarizes these canonical frequency bands and their associated mental states<sup><a href="#CR12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR12">12</a></sup>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig1"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12706009_41598_2025_28070_Fig1_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/54f389effe1f/41598_2025_28070_Fig1_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO1" height="479" width="727" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>EEG frequency bands and corresponding cognitive states.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par5">Abnormalities such as elevated theta/delta activity and reduced alpha/beta power have been consistently observed in AD patients<sup><a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">13</a>,<a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">14</a></sup>. Consequently, EEG-based biomarkers hold immense promise for non-invasive and early-stage Alzheimer’s detection. Alzheimer’s disease is also accompanied by emotional and affective disturbances including apathy, anxiety, depression, irritability, and emotional lability<sup><a href="#CR15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR15">15</a></sup>. These symptoms further degrade quality of life and complicate caregiving. Recent EEG studies have explored emotional-state recognition in both virtual and physical environments, demonstrating that EEG dynamics can reliably encode affective states<sup><a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>,<a href="#CR17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR17">17</a></sup>. Although the present work focuses on diagnostic classification, the integration of emotional correlates could, in future, enhance multimodal AD assessment frameworks.</p>
<p id="Par6"> Standard processing pipelines typically involve data preparation, filtering, segmentation, feature extraction, and classification<sup><a href="#CR18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR18">18</a>–<a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a></sup>. However, methodological diversity in preprocessing, feature engineering, and model interpretability continues to hinder clinical reproducibility. Table <a href="#Tab2" class="usa-link">2</a> summarizes key prior works, highlighting their main approaches and limitations.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par7">As evident from Table <a href="#Tab2" class="usa-link">2</a>, prior studies have often relied on isolated feature-learning schemes lacking interpretability, cross-subject robustness, or rigorous clinical validation. While handcrafted features (spectral, statistical, and connectivity) capture domain-specific patterns, few studies have combined them with deep-learned features to achieve better classification accuracy. This gap motivates the development of a unified, explainable, and generalizable framework capable of learning both interpretable and high-level representations from EEG data.</p>
<p id="Par8">While the primary evaluation of NeuroFusionNet was conducted on the OpenNeuro eyes-closed dataset (ds004504), reliable clinical translation demands testing under varied demographic conditions. Hence, two additional datasets were including the OpenNeuro ds006036 (eyes-open) for assessing condition robustness, and the OSF EEG dataset have been used for external validation.</p>
<p id="Par9">A broader survey of additional EEG datasets related to Alzheimer’s disease is summarized in Table <a href="#Tab5" class="usa-link">5</a>. These datasets differ in demographics, sampling rates, and recording durations. Table <a href="#Tab6" class="usa-link">6</a> provides the comparison of the top performing methods on AD detection using EEG signals on different datasets. </p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="Tab5"><h3 class="obj_head">Table 5.</h3>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><section id="Sec2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Key contributions and novelty</h3>
<p id="Par10">To clearly delineate the novelty of our work and its distinction from prior EEG-based Alzheimer’s frameworks, we summarize the main contributions of NeuroFusionNet as follows:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p id="Par11">Combined both handcrafted (spectral, statistical, and connectivity) and CNN-derived deep features to achieve both interpretability and abstraction.</p></li>
<li><p id="Par12">Employed Pearson correlation analysis, PSO-based selection, and bottleneck fusion to maximize complementarity and minimize redundancy unlike prior naive concatenation approaches.</p></li>
<li><p id="Par13">Integrated Grad-CAM and SHAP back-projection to link AI reasoning with neurophysiological regions and rhythms.</p></li>
<li><p id="Par14">Comprehensive robustness validation: Demonstrates condition and site generalization via ds004504, ds006036, and OSF datasets.</p></li>
<li><p id="Par15">Achieved <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq30"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/204af1d4eeef/d33e1007.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1007" alt="Inline graphic"></span>21% improvement in signal-to-noise ratio (8.12<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq31"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/3419b6f67981/d33e1011.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1011" alt="Inline graphic"></span>9.89 dB) and 21% spectral stability gain, ensuring reliable EEG interpretation.</p></li>
<li><p id="Par16">Demographic fairness: Subgroup (gender, age) analyses confirm unbiased performance (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq32"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/558180c08a7e/d33e1018.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1018" alt="Inline graphic"></span>).</p></li>
<li><p id="Par17">0.94M parameters, 4.1MB footprint, and 6.5 ms/sample CPU latency support real-time, portable EEG use.</p></li>
<li><p id="Par18">Outperformed classical (SVM, RF, KNN, XGBoost) and recent deep models, highlighting hybrid deep–shallow superiority.</p></li>
</ul>
<p>The following sections present the methodological design, preprocessing pipeline, feature level fusion, and interpretability.</p></section></section><section id="Sec3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Proposed methodology</h2>
<p id="Par19">The proposed NeuroFusionNet framework follows a systematic six-stage pipeline that includes Data acquisition and preparation, EEG preprocessing, Feature extraction, Feature fusion and model construction, Training and Evaluation along with interpretability. This modular design ensures transparency, reproducibility, and flexibility for deployment across multiple EEG datasets. Figure <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a> provides flow diagram of the AD detection method proposed in this research.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig2"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 2.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12706009_41598_2025_28070_Fig2_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/74b8d91323ba/41598_2025_28070_Fig2_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO2" height="415" width="668" alt="Fig. 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Overall workflow of the proposed NeuroFusionNet framework. The architecture integrates hybrid feature fusion, robust preprocessing, and SHAP/Grad-CAM explainability modules for clinically interpretable Alzheimer’s classification.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="Sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Data acquisition and preparation</h3>
<p id="Par20">To ensure comprehensive evaluation and generalization, we utilized three publicly available EEG datasets representing distinct recording conditions and demographic distributions: the OpenNeuro ds004504 (eyes-closed), OpenNeuro ds006036 (eyes-open), and the OSF EEG dataset for external validation. This multi-dataset design enables condition and site-level robustness assessment under heterogeneous acquisition protocols.</p>
<section id="Sec5"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">OpenNeuro ds004504 (eyes-closed):</h4>
<p id="Par21">This primary dataset contains EEG recordings from 88 subjects (AD: 36, FTD: 23, CN: 29) publicly available at <a href="https://openneuro.org/datasets/ds004504/versions/1.0.8" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://openneuro.org/datasets/ds004504/versions/1.0.8</a>. Each participant underwent neuropsychological evaluation using the Mini-Mental State Examination (MMSE), with average scores of 30 (CN), 22 (FTD), and 18 (AD). Recordings were performed using 18 electrodes positioned according to the international 10–20 system with A1–A2 references and maintained impedance below 5 k<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq33"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/455934dd5b37/d33e1055.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1055" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Signals were captured at 500 Hz using a Nihon Kohden EEG-2100 system during eyes-closed resting conditions.</p></section><section id="Sec6"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">OpenNeuro ds006036 (eyes-open):</h4>
<p id="Par22">This dataset includes EEG recordings from the same 88 participants under eyes-open resting conditions. It enables direct comparison of physiological and cognitive state variations. The acquisition protocol matched ds004504 in montage and impedance, with a 500 Hz sampling rate to ensure consistent temporal resolution.</p></section><section id="Sec7"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">OSF EEG dataset (external validation):</h4>
<p id="Par23">For independent generalization testing, we included the OSF EEG dataset (<a href="https://osf.io/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://osf.io/</a>), containing data from 109 subjects (AD and Cognitively Normal groups). Recordings were performed using 21-channel systems sampled at 512 Hz under eyes-closed resting conditions. This dataset differs in demographics and instrumentation, offering an ideal test set for assessing cross-site transferability and model robustness. The structure of EEG data across all datasets can be represented as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ1"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/9566b47863c6/d33e1071.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1071" alt="graphic file with name d33e1071.gif"></td>
<td class="label">1</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq34"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/c35695bcba8e/d33e1076.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1076" alt="Inline graphic"></span> denotes the EEG recording of the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq35"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/f829fb0adcf6/d33e1080.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1080" alt="Inline graphic"></span> subject, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq36"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/e1842c352784/d33e1084.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1084" alt="Inline graphic"></span> is the number of recordings, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq37"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/2608698f928f/d33e1089.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1089" alt="Inline graphic"></span> the number of temporal samples, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq38"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/73bf1af2851c/d33e1093.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1093" alt="Inline graphic"></span> the number of EEG channels. All signals were preprocessing using notch filtering, ICA-based artifact removal, and z-score normalization. EEG datasets for dementia diagnosis are imbalanced, often containing fewer AD or FTD samples compared to cognitively normal controls. To mitigate this imbalance problem, we employed the Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique (SMOTE)<sup><a href="#CR38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR38">38</a></sup>, which generates synthetic feature vectors for minority classes via linear interpolation between nearest neighbors. Unlike random oversampling, SMOTE preserves local structure and class boundaries, reducing the risk of overfitting. Table <a href="#Tab7" class="usa-link">7</a> summarize class distributions before and after applying SMOTE on the ds004504 dataset. SMOTE yielded approximately uniform representation across classes, stabilizing convergence and ensuring equitable gradient updates during training. To avoid overfitting, SMOTE was applied strictly within training folds during cross-validation; validation and test folds remained unchanged. This approach aligns with best practices in biomedical machine learning by maintaining independent evaluation and unbiased model assessment.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="Tab7"><h5 class="obj_head">Table 7.</h5>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section><section id="Sec8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Preprocessing</h3>
<p id="Par24">EEG signals are susceptible to non-neural artifacts such as ocular blinks, muscle activity, and electrode drifts, which can distort frequency-domain characteristics relevant for dementia classification. To guarantee reproducibility and minimize observer bias, the NeuroFusionNet pipeline employs a fully standardized and semi-automated denoising strategy comprising three sequential stages: (1) 1–45 Hz band-pass filtering to suppress DC drift and high-frequency interference, (2) Artifact Subspace Reconstruction (ASR) for transient artifact attenuation, and (3) Independent Component Analysis (ICA) using the Infomax algorithm to isolate and remove ocular and myogenic components. ICA components with kurtosis&gt; 5, skewness &gt; 2, or variance exceeding ± 3 SD from the mean were automatically flagged as non-neural. Components showing strong correlation with EOG reference channels or muscular topographies were excluded. To further enhance reproducibility, the Multiple Artifact Rejection Algorithm (MARA)<sup><a href="#CR39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR39">39</a></sup> was employed to validate and refine component classification. All thresholds were applied uniformly across subjects and datasets to ensure consistent denoising. The effectiveness of preprocessing was quantified using the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) and Residual Artifact Power (RAP) metrics summarized in Table <a href="#Tab8" class="usa-link">8</a>. This standardized pipeline achieved reproducible noise suppression while preserving relevant neural information.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par25">Across all datasets, SNR increased by roughly 21 % while RAP decreased by 36–38 %, confirming that ICA–MARA effectively suppresses artifacts without distorting neural oscillations. To further quantify denoising reliability, we measured the Signal-to-Artifact Ratio (SAR), Power Spectral Stability (PSS), and Channel Retention Rate (CRR) on 88 subjects from ds004504 (Table <a href="#Tab9" class="usa-link">9</a>). The results verify that spectral integrity and spatial consistency were preserved after preprocessing. Figure <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4</a> illustrates the progression from raw EEG to fully cleaned signals, showing suppression of ocular and EMG spikes while retaining alpha–theta rhythms. Complementarily, robustness under synthetic noise perturbations (Gaussian <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq42"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/d6612150c52f/d33e1310.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1310" alt="Inline graphic"></span>–0.2) was tested; Figure <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a> depicts that model accuracy declined by less than 2.1 % under high-noise conditions, confirming strong noise resilience.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig4"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12706009_41598_2025_28070_Fig4_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/0aedeb3b61fd/41598_2025_28070_Fig4_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO4" height="443" width="730" alt="Fig. 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Illustration of preprocessing robustness: (<strong>a</strong>) raw EEG with ocular / EMG artifacts, (<strong>b</strong>) signal after ASR denoising, and (<strong>c</strong>) final cleaned EEG after ICA component removal. Physiological alpha–theta rhythms are preserved while transient artifacts are eliminated.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig3"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12706009_41598_2025_28070_Fig3_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/bbe83e9cd577/41598_2025_28070_Fig3_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO3" height="488" width="668" alt="Fig. 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Model stability under simulated EEG noise. Boxplot shows accuracy distributions across five folds for Gaussian noise levels (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq43"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/d6612150c52f/d33e1323.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1323" alt="Inline graphic"></span>–0.2).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par26">Quantitative and visual results confirm that the proposed denoising pipeline enhances SNR by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq44"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/cae9e73346b2/d33e1348.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1348" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, improves spectral stability, and maintains spatial fidelity establishing a reliable foundation for subsequent feature extraction. Following validation of preprocessing quality, the next step was to formalize each transformation within the standardized workflow to ensure reproducibility. EEG recordings were further processed through the following steps, each contributing to the final denoised and normalized input used for feature extraction and classification. A 4th-order Butterworth filter (0.5–45 Hz) removed low-frequency drifts and high-frequency noise. Its response is defined as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ2"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/429a505d7f13/d33e1352.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1352" alt="graphic file with name d33e1352.gif"></td>
<td class="label">2</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq45"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/0fa42dae2c49/d33e1357.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1357" alt="Inline graphic"></span> is the angular frequency, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq46"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/675921a05b97/d33e1361.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1361" alt="Inline graphic"></span> the cutoff frequency, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq47"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/d33b0cc98dfd/d33e1365.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1365" alt="Inline graphic"></span> the filter order.</p>
<p id="Par27">Signals were re-referenced using the average of mastoid electrodes (A1 and A2) to reduce bias and enhance inter-channel comparability. ASR<sup><a href="#CR40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR40">40</a></sup> removed transient high-amplitude artifacts by projecting contaminated segments onto a clean covariance-derived subspace. EEG data were decomposed as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ3"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/e5bf3b573a72/d33e1375.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1375" alt="graphic file with name d33e1375.gif"></td>
<td class="label">3</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq48"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/c35695bcba8e/d33e1380.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1380" alt="Inline graphic"></span> is the observed EEG matrix, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq49"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/b5df4d6bf12f/d33e1384.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1384" alt="Inline graphic"></span> the mixing matrix, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq50"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/ed16e193612f/d33e1388.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1388" alt="Inline graphic"></span> the independent sources. Non-neural components were identified via EEGLAB and removed using the criteria described above. Each cleaned EEG signal was standardized using z-score normalization:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ4"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/70777031e492/d33e1393.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1393" alt="graphic file with name d33e1393.gif"></td>
<td class="label">4</td>
</tr></table>
<p>with mean and standard deviation computed as</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ5"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/db58cf6d01b9/d33e1398.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1398" alt="graphic file with name d33e1398.gif"></td>
<td class="label">5</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ6"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/5b91d764d217/d33e1402.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1402" alt="graphic file with name d33e1402.gif"></td>
<td class="label">6</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Normalized signals were divided into non-overlapping segments of 1 000 samples:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ7"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/bf57d6c752e1/d33e1407.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1407" alt="graphic file with name d33e1407.gif"></td>
<td class="label">7</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq51"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/e84799cc6686/d33e1412.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1412" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq52"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/63150f823f25/d33e1416.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1416" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq53"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/8349da772c7f/d33e1421.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1421" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p>
<p id="Par28">The proposed preprocessing framework integrates domain-standard filtering, referencing, and statistical denoising validated both quantitatively (Tables <a href="#Tab8" class="usa-link">8</a>, <a href="#Tab9" class="usa-link">9</a>) and visually (Figures  <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>, <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4</a>, <a href="#Fig5" class="usa-link">5</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig5"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12706009_41598_2025_28070_Fig5_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/c60d00a472d3/41598_2025_28070_Fig5_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO5" height="327" width="667" alt="Fig. 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Comparison of raw and normalized EEG signals for channels Cz and Fz. Normalization stabilizes amplitude variance across segments.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Feature extraction</h3>
<p id="Par29">After robust preprocessing, both handcrafted and automatically learned features were extracted from each clean and segmented EEG window <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq54"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/9c8c60a85aac/d33e1453.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1453" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. This dual-stream design captures complementary information domain-specific descriptors from handcrafted analysis and nonlinear temporal abstractions from deep learning. The complete feature extraction process is summarized below.</p>
<section id="Sec10"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Handcrafted feature extraction</h4>
<p id="Par30">Each EEG segment was passed through a multi-domain analytical pipeline involving statistical, spectral, wavelet, and entropy-based descriptors. Features were computed channel-wise and concatenated into a unified vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq55"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/e191075510aa/d33e1461.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1461" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Table <a href="#Tab10" class="usa-link">10</a> lists the extracted feature domains. Power spectral density (PSD), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq56"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/d2024a28c65c/d33e1468.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1468" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, was estimated using Welch’s method, and the logarithmic bandpower was computed for canonical EEG frequency bands (delta, theta, alpha, beta, gamma) as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ8"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/879d6171c6ae/d33e1472.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1472" alt="graphic file with name d33e1472.gif"></td>
<td class="label">8</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq57"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/d2024a28c65c/d33e1477.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1477" alt="Inline graphic"></span> denotes the spectral power, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq58"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/d804db09186f/d33e1482.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1482" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq59"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/ecbd60763b94/d33e1486.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1486" alt="Inline graphic"></span> represent the lower and upper frequency limits, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq60"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/cd8253eb5575/d33e1490.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1490" alt="Inline graphic"></span> is a stabilization constant. Temporal-frequency decomposition was conducted using Daubechies-4 (db4) wavelets up to level 5. From each coefficient set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq61"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/4e12f446d693/d33e1494.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1494" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, we extracted the mean and standard deviation:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ9"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/6df8142f6ece/d33e1498.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1498" alt="graphic file with name d33e1498.gif"></td>
<td class="label">9</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Higher-order moments such as skewness and kurtosis were further estimated to capture waveform asymmetry and peakedness:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ10"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/7f9e2d3c1d53/d33e1503.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1503" alt="graphic file with name d33e1503.gif"></td>
<td class="label">10</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Finally, permutation entropy quantified temporal complexity:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ11"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/6e6633834f1a/d33e1509.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1509" alt="graphic file with name d33e1509.gif"></td>
<td class="label">11</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq62"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/02faa2a54e6d/d33e1514.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1514" alt="Inline graphic"></span> is the probability of each ordinal pattern of length <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq63"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/1366073af62f/d33e1518.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1518" alt="Inline graphic"></span>.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab10/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="Sec11"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Automated feature extraction (1D-CNN):</h4>
<p id="Par31">We propose a lightweight 1D Convolutional Neural Network (1D-CNN) for automated detection of features from multichannel EEG signals. It consists of three convolutional layers followed by max pooling and a global average pooling layer for dimensionality reduction. Each convolutional block includes batch normalization and ReLU activation function:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ12"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/dcfaeca861ab/d33e1561.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1561" alt="graphic file with name d33e1561.gif"></td>
<td class="label">12</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq64"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/4bac6fc41765/d33e1566.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1566" alt="Inline graphic"></span> is the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq65"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/c45d69bf42cc/d33e1570.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1570" alt="Inline graphic"></span> feature map, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq66"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/1c5f8b8121af/d33e1574.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1574" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq67"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/9046e74c5a69/d33e1578.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1578" alt="Inline graphic"></span> are learnable parameters, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq68"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/cfeb3fe62652/d33e1583.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1583" alt="Inline graphic"></span> denotes batch normalization. The 1D-CNN architecture utilizes the temporal nature of EEG data, avoiding the computational burden of 2D convolutions while preserving interpretability. The number of filters (32–128) and kernel sizes (3, 5, 7) were tuned through structured grid search and validated via 5-fold cross-validation. The configuration of 64 filters and kernel size 5 achieved optimal validation accuracy and generalization. Dropout rates (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq69"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/cd0ea39ffbd5/d33e1587.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1587" alt="Inline graphic"></span>–0.4) were concurrently optimized to balance regularization and representational capacity, as further confirmed in the ablation results (Table <a href="#Tab19" class="usa-link">19</a>).</p>

<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">64</td>

</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab19/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par32">To avoid overfitting while retaining multi-scale temporal features, the final CNN depth was limited to three convolutional blocks. This configuration demonstrated strong reproducibility and generalization across all datasets. To justify the 1D-CNN design choice, a controlled comparison was performed against an equivalent 2D-CNN architecture under identical training protocols. As shown in Table <a href="#Tab11" class="usa-link">11</a> and Fig. <a href="#Fig6" class="usa-link">6</a>, the 1D-CNN achieved higher accuracy, faster inference, and lower variance. While the 2D-CNN could model inter-channel dependencies, it incurred 62% higher computational cost and tended to overfit due to limited EEG samples. Thus, temporal dependencies were prioritized over spatial correlations for short-segment EEG analysis.</p>

<p id="Par36">This hybrid feature engineering strategy integrates interpretable handcrafted EEG descriptors with deep learned temporal embeddings. By combining spectral, wavelet, and statistical markers with CNN-derived patterns, NeuroFusionNet leverages both neuroscientific priors and data-driven abstraction. The resulting PCA-compressed feature set provides a balanced, noise-robust representation that enhances both accuracy and interpretability–serving as the foundation for the Model Construction phase described next (Tables <a href="#Tab12" class="usa-link">12</a>, <a href="#Tab13" class="usa-link">13</a>).</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab13/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section></section><section id="Sec12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Classification network and optimization strategy</h3>
<p id="Par37"> Final feature vector obtained by concatenating handcrafted and deep-learned features passed to the classification stage of NeuroFusionNet which employs a deep neural network (DNN) designed to learn class-discriminative EEG representations in an interpretable and computationally efficient manner. It consists of regularization, normalization, and residual connections to stabilize gradient flow and mitigate overfitting during training. A detailed layer-wise architecture of the proposed network is summarized in Table <a href="#Tab15" class="usa-link">15</a>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="Tab15"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 15.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab15/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par38">Let <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq95"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/7ac12113345e/d33e1953.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1953" alt="Inline graphic"></span> represent the PCA-reduced feature vector for the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq96"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/f829fb0adcf6/d33e1957.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1957" alt="Inline graphic"></span> EEG segment. It is input to the classifier as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ17"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/28a1c75417d2/d33e1961.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1961" alt="graphic file with name d33e1961.gif"></td>
<td class="label">17</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The DNN consists of five fully connected (dense) layers, each followed by batch normalization (BN), dropout, and a non-linear activation. A skip connection between the first and third dense layers facilitates residual learning and promotes smooth gradient propagation. The transformation at the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq97"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/c45d69bf42cc/d33e1966.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1966" alt="Inline graphic"></span> dense layer is expressed as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ18"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/c8b8f92ad885/d33e1970.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1970" alt="graphic file with name d33e1970.gif"></td>
<td class="label">18</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq98"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/75a72dbb8086/d33e1976.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1976" alt="Inline graphic"></span> denotes the LeakyReLU activation, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq99"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/de172775afd3/d33e1980.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1980" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq100"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/9046e74c5a69/d33e1984.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1984" alt="Inline graphic"></span> are learnable parameters. Dropout layers (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq101"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/37a1d78ddbdb/d33e1988.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1988" alt="Inline graphic"></span>–<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq102"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/9860102e4edb/d33e1992.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1992" alt="Inline graphic"></span>) control overfitting, while the output layer applies the softmax activation to yield probabilistic class scores:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ19"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/22ff8e1765b4/d33e1996.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e1996" alt="graphic file with name d33e1996.gif"></td>
<td class="label">19</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The network is trained by minimizing the categorical cross-entropy loss with <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq103"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/89ac728b71d6/d33e2002.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2002" alt="Inline graphic"></span> weight regularization:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ20"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/f1d9bbc59912/d33e2006.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2006" alt="graphic file with name d33e2006.gif"></td>
<td class="label">20</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq104"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/27aca44b50ef/d33e2011.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2011" alt="Inline graphic"></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq105"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/fd4d81d1438b/d33e2015.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2015" alt="Inline graphic"></span> represent the true and predicted class probabilities, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq106"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/95c7c0c413ff/d33e2019.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2019" alt="Inline graphic"></span> denotes the regularization coefficient. Optimization was performed using the Adam optimizer with an initial learning rate of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq107"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/1d04ceba2266/d33e2023.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2023" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. A dynamic learning rate adjustment was employed through the ReduceLROnPlateau scheduler (factor = 0.5, patience = 10), which automatically reduced the learning rate by half after 10 consecutive epochs without validation improvement. Typically, this mechanism lowered the learning rate to approximately <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq108"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/22e884410da8/d33e2028.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2028" alt="Inline graphic"></span> between epochs 20 and 40, enabling stable convergence and enhanced generalization. This schedule was consistently maintained across all datasets and cross-validation folds to ensure reproducibility.</p>
<p id="Par39">A structured grid search was conducted to identify optimal model parameters, covering convolutional filters <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq114"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/bcb327efa19d/d33e2121.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2121" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, kernel sizes <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq115"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/25e4382903af/d33e2125.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2125" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, and dropout rates <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq116"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/7c33697570d8/d33e2129.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2129" alt="Inline graphic"></span>. Each configuration was evaluated under five-fold cross-validation on the OpenNeuro training set to measure validation accuracy and model stability. The configuration of 64 filters, kernel size = 5, and dropout = 0.3–0.4 achieved the best trade-off between generalization and computational efficiency. All tuned hyperparameters are summarized in Table <a href="#Tab14" class="usa-link">14</a>, and the final layer-wise network design is described in Table <a href="#Tab15" class="usa-link">15</a>. The architecture of NeuroFusionNet is specifically designed to model the high-dimensional, nonlinear dependencies within fused EEG features while maintaining interpretability and training stability. It comprises five dense layers interleaved with normalization, non-linear activation, and dropout layers. The inclusion of a skip connection enhances feature propagation and facilitates residual learning, minimizing gradient degradation. A concise layer-wise description is presented in Table <a href="#Tab15" class="usa-link">15</a>.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab14/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par40">The modular design of NeuroFusionNet is presented in the Fig. <a href="#Fig7" class="usa-link">7</a>. Each component block represents a distinct transformation stage, highlighting how low-level features from Dense-1 are reintroduced into Dense-3 via the skip pathway. This residual mapping provides hierarchical representation learning and enhances robustness to noisy EEG data. To prevent premature convergence or oscillatory loss behavior, a dynamic learning rate schedule was applied through ReduceLROnPlateau. The learning rate was reduced to half after extended periods of validation loss stagnation (patience = 10). In practice, this led to a controlled decay from <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq117"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/1d04ceba2266/d33e2158.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2158" alt="Inline graphic"></span> to approximately <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq118"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/22e884410da8/d33e2162.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2162" alt="Inline graphic"></span> around epochs 20–40<sup><a href="#CR41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR41">41</a></sup>. This adaptive decay accelerates early-stage learning and fine-tunes convergence during later epochs, yielding more stable optimization and improved generalization across EEG datasets.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig7"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/9e85e3df6f3d/41598_2025_28070_Fig7_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO7" height="981" width="694" alt="Fig. 7"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Modular architecture of NeuroFusionNet with skip connection from Dense-1 to Dense-3.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par41">To improve the generalization, the OpenNeuro (ds004504) dataset was partitioned at the <em>subject level</em>. A stratified assignment maintained class balance across five folds, with each fold comprising 70% training, 15% validation, and 15% testing subjects. This ensures that no EEG segment from a single participant appears in more than one subset and prevents temporal or spatial leakage. The model achieving the highest validation F1-score within each fold has been used for testing. The overall subject distribution is summarized in Table <a href="#Tab16" class="usa-link">16</a>.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab16/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par42">This rigorous data-partitioning framework ensures statistical reliability and generalization under limited-sample EEG conditions. Averaged metrics across five folds (mean ± standard deviation) are reported in subsequent sections as reliable indicators of model performance and reproducibility. In summary, the proposed classification module of NeuroFusionNet unifies efficient optimization, adaptive learning rate scheduling, and stratified cross-validation within a residual deep-learning framework.</p></section></section><section id="Sec13"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results and discussion</h2>
<p id="Par43">In clinical neuroscience applications, model interpretability is extremely important to gain trust among practitioners. To improve interpretability, we incorporated XAI techniques including SHAP and Grad-CAM to reveal model decision patterns. We conducted experiments on three datasets including OpenNeuro ds004504 (eyes-closed) as the primary dataset, OpenNeuro ds006036 (eyes-open) to assess condition robustness, and the independent OSF dataset for validation. </p>
<p id="Par47">An extensive ablation study was conducted to evaluate the effectiveness of key components of the suggested AD detection pipeline. We first evaluated the impact of different feature construction strategies and preprocessing steps (SMOTE, PCA). Combining domain-specific handcrafted features with CNN-based deep embeddings led to a marked increase in classification accuracy, as shown in Table <a href="#Tab17" class="usa-link">17</a>. SMOTE-based oversampling and PCA-based dimensionality reduction contribute to class balance and improve the model’s generalization performance. The proposed model found a test accuracy of 94.27%, which suggests good generalization robustness over the three EEG classes. The macro-averaged precision, recall and F1-score were all roughly 0.94, as shown in Table <a href="#Tab18" class="usa-link">18</a>. Figure <a href="#Fig12" class="usa-link">12</a> represents Feature scatter plot illustrating separability of AD, FTD, and HC classes using three key extracted features: Delta band power, Mean amplitude, and Standard Deviation. Figure <a href="#Fig10" class="usa-link">10</a> illustrates training dynamics, showing stable convergence and minimal overfitting. To further optimize the architecture, we conducted an ablation study evaluating the influence of various identical-parameter configurations on model performance. To empirically validate the selected hyperparameter configuration, an ablation analysis was conducted on the OpenNeuro ds004504 dataset. Each parameter (number of layers, dropout rate, learning rate, batch size) was varied independently while keeping all others fixed to assess its impact on model accuracy. The averaged results over three runs are summarized in Table <a href="#Tab19" class="usa-link">19</a>. We observed that increasing the network depth to four hidden layers (256–128–64–32) consistently improved accuracy. A moderate dropout rate of 0.3 yielded the best generalization, striking a balance between regularization and learning stability. Similarly, a lower learning rate of 0.0005 facilitated more stable convergence, while a batch size of 32 outperformed smaller or larger batch sizes by ensuring optimal gradient estimation and batch-level diversity. These insights confirm both the robustness and transparency of our proposed model.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab18/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig12"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 12.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/5b031e1b45ab/41598_2025_28070_Fig12_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO12" height="496" width="669" alt="Fig. 12"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig12/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Feature scatter plot (ds004504 eyes-closed dataset).</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig10"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 10.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/9c145b03a83d/41598_2025_28070_Fig10_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO10" height="1091" width="684" alt="Fig. 10"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig10/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(<strong>a</strong>) Accuracy and (<strong>b</strong>) loss during training and validation phases.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par48">As shown in Table <a href="#Tab19" class="usa-link">19</a>, the grid-search procedure identified a configuration with four dense layers, dropout = 0.3, learning rate = <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq122"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/26683810b3b7/d33e2999.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e2999" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, and batch size = 32 as the most stable and accurate setup across three validation runs. These hyperparameter values were therefore adopted as the default configuration for all subsequent experiments reported in Tables <a href="#Tab14" class="usa-link">14</a> and <a href="#Tab15" class="usa-link">15</a>. We performed an extensive cross-validation and regularization analysis on the OpenNeuro (ds004504, ds006036) and OSF datasets. A stratified 5-fold cross-validation protocol was applied, ensuring proportional representation of AD, FTD, and CN classes in each fold. The mean and standard deviation of key metrics across folds are summarized in Table <a href="#Tab20" class="usa-link">20</a>. Low values of standard deviation indicates stable performance across folds, confirming that the model’s high accuracy is not due to the data partitioning. To reduce the effect of the overfitting, we employed multiple regularization techniques: (1) dropout layers (rate = 0.3) between dense blocks, (2) L2 weight decay (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq123"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/f540fd1acca9/d33e3013.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3013" alt="Inline graphic"></span>), and (3) early stopping with a patience of 10 epochs. The near-uniform fold-level performance (SD = 0.28%) and consistent F1/AUC scores demonstrate that NeuroFusionNet’s high accuracy is reproducible and not a consequence of overfitting or data leakage. NeuroFusionNet integrates both handcrafted and deep learning-based feature, however, its architecture has been optimized to ensure computational efficiency and scalability. The model comprises approximately 0.94 million trainable parameters, with a total storage of 4.1 MB required. Inference time was empirically measured to be 6.5 milliseconds per sample on a CPU (Intel Core i7, 2.8 GHz) which supports its real time implementation. Table <a href="#Tab21" class="usa-link">21</a> presents a comparative analysis of NeuroFusionNet against two representative baselines frequently employed in EEG-based Alzheimer’s disease classification: a standard 1D Convolutional Neural Network (1D-CNN) and a Transformer-based model<sup><a href="#CR46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR46">46</a></sup>. The results clearly demonstrate that NeuroFusionNet achieved high classification accuracy with reduced computational cost. We further evaluated the deployment feasibility of NeuroFusionNet in real-world clinical settings. While achieving high classification accuracy is critical, practical usability requires low-latency inference, minimal hardware resources, and integration into existing EEG acquisition pipelines. To further demonstrate deployability in clinical and portable EEG environments, we summarize the deployment configuration and observed inference performance in Table  <a href="#Tab26" class="usa-link">26</a>. NeuroFusionNet achieves sub-10 ms latency on a mid-range CPU, enabling real-time decision support without the need for high-end GPUs. The lightweight parameter count and memory footprint make it suitable for bedside systems and embedded clinical workstations.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab26/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par49">In terms of integration, the entire pipeline (preprocessing, feature extraction, NeuroFusionNet inference, and post-processing) was containerized using Docker to ensure reproducibility and easy installation across clinical workstations. Furthermore, the model was tested on streaming EEG input, where latency remained below 20 ms end-to-end, well within the clinical threshold for interactive diagnostic systems. This supports the feasibility of incorporating NeuroFusionNet into neurology clinics and research centers without specialized hardware. To ensure usability by non-technical clinicians, we also designed a prototype clinician-facing interface that provides tabular summaries and interpretable visualizations such as SHAP and Grad-CAM heatmaps at the patient level (see Fig. <a href="#Fig16" class="usa-link">16</a> and Table  <a href="#Tab29" class="usa-link">29</a>). This improves accessibility by allowing physicians to interpret predictions in terms of brain regions and EEG frequency bands, rather than abstract machine learning scores.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab29/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par50">These deployment-oriented evaluations confirm that NeuroFusionNet is not only accurate but also computationally efficient, clinically interpretable, and deployable in real-time EEG workflows. Figure  <a href="#Fig8" class="usa-link">8</a> shows the confusion matrix which shows the high class-wise prediction accuracy with low misclassification across classes. Class-wise ROC curves presented in the Fig. <a href="#Fig9" class="usa-link">9</a> to evaluate the classifier’s discriminative capabilities; all classes had Area Under Curve (AUC) values greater than 0.96. To ensure robustness and prevent information leakage, cosine similarity was computed between training and test feature vectors. The mean similarity score was only 0.0063, reflecting negligible overlap and strong generalization. To enhance the interpretability of the proposed deep learning model, Shapley Additive Explanations (SHAP)<sup><a href="#CR47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR47">47</a></sup> were utilized. SHAP Kernel Explainer was used to determine how each feature contributed to the model’s predictions. Figure <a href="#Fig11" class="usa-link">11</a> presents the SHAP summary plot, highlighting the most influential features that drive the classification outcomes, thus providing transparency into the decision-making process.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig8"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 8.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/47ac3b0af032/41598_2025_28070_Fig8_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO8" height="508" width="645" alt="Fig. 8"></p>

For each principal component <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq124"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/b56f74939f20/d33e3055.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3055" alt="Inline graphic"></span> with loading vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq125"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/f921082c01c8/d33e3059.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3059" alt="Inline graphic"></span>, the contribution of an original feature <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq126"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/4504debe7471/d33e3063.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3063" alt="Inline graphic"></span> was reconstructed as:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ21"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/2d69a0b86162/d33e3067.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3067" alt="graphic file with name d33e3067.gif"></td>
<td class="label">21</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq127"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/a0b2d4478e77/d33e3073.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3073" alt="Inline graphic"></span> represents the attribution score of the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq128"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/66eb55315174/d33e3077.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3077" alt="Inline graphic"></span> principal component, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq129"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/68d48bd8b9e9/d33e3081.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3081" alt="Inline graphic"></span> is the weight of feature <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq130"><img class="inline" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/4504debe7471/d33e3085.gif" loading="lazy" id="d33e3085" alt="Inline graphic"></span> in that component. This formulation allows identification of the most contributing handcrafted features and EEG channels contributing to each prediction, thereby linking model explanations directly to neurophysiological sources and improving clinical interpretability.</p>

<td align="left" colspan="1" rowspan="1">10.0</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab24/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par52">These findings not only validate the predictive performance and explainability of the proposed architecture, but also reveal the effectiveness of combining domain relevant and data-driven representations for AD detection. Although hybrid feature strategies have been explored in past EEG classification works, our approach introduces a novel and more principled integration mechanism. Unlike prior studies that rely on direct concatenation or early fusion of handcrafted and deep features, NeuroFusionNet applies a mid-level bottleneck fusion after feature selection. Handcrafted features are first selected through Pearson-correlation analysis and PSO-based feature selection to remove features with high correlation. These optimized handcrafted features are then concatenated with CNN based automated features. Furthermore, unlike prior works that treat hybrid features as a black box, our pipeline incorporates SHAP-based explainability to interpret feature contributions at both global and instance levels. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first EEG-based Alzheimer’s disease detection framework that jointly optimizes fusion depth, dimensionality reduction, and interpretability within a clinically aligned workflow.</p>
<p id="Par53">To enhance clinical relevance and figure clarity, interpretability results were reorganized for readability and medical insight. Beyond numerical attribution, the SHAP–PCA analysis revealed physiologically coherent patterns. High values of <em>theta</em> over frontal and central electrodes (F3, F4, Cz) and reduced <em>alpha</em> power in parieto-occipital sites (Pz, O1, O2) emerged as dominant discriminators between cognitive states. Entropy and wavelet-derived metrics from temporal parietal channels (T5, T6, P3) further reflect declining neural complexity and local desynchronization. These observations confirm that the model’s SHAP attributions correspond to clinically recognized neurophysiological abnormalities, strengthening the translational validity of NeuroFusionNet.</p>
<p id="Par56">Figure <a href="#Fig13" class="usa-link">13</a> further illustrates the SHAP beeswarm plot, showcasing the top 20 Principal Components (PCs) that contribute towards improved classification. Each point represents a single test instance, colored by feature value and spread horizontally based on the SHAP value (impact on output). Features that show a larger distribution are more variable among samples, which suggests that they are important for differentiating between AD, FTD, and HC. To further evaluate the separability of the learned EEG feature representations, we applied t-distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding (t-SNE) to the final fused feature set.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig13"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 13.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/0289d013e614/41598_2025_28070_Fig13_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO13" height="828" width="552" alt="Fig. 13"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig13/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>SHAP summary plot illustrating feature importance in physiologically meaningful terms.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par57">To evaluate the robustness of NeuroFusionNet under different physiological conditions, we tested the model on the OpenNeuro eyes-open dataset (ds006036). This dataset includes the same 88 participants as the eyes-closed dataset, but with photic stimulation and eyes-open resting state EEG. Accuracy decreased slightly compared to the eyes-closed condition due to increased noise and variability in alpha activity, yet the model maintained strong performance across classes. Table  <a href="#Tab22" class="usa-link">22</a> shows that NeuroFusionNet remains stable across resting state conditions, with only a minor performance drop in the eyes-open setting. This robustness against physiological variability is critical for ensuring reliable performance in real-world clinical deployments. We evaluated NeuroFusionNet on the independent OSF EEG dataset, which differs in demographics, acquisition device, and sampling frequency (128 Hz). The proposed model achieved strong and balanced performance across AD, MCI, and HC classes. As presented in the Table <a href="#Tab23" class="usa-link">23</a>, NeuroFusionNet maintained balanced precision, recall, and F1-scores across all classes. Although performance was slightly lower than on the OpenNeuro datasets likely due to shorter recording duration and lower sampling frequency the results confirm that the framework generalizes effectively to independent datasets with differing acquisition protocols, strengthening its clinical relevance.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab23/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par58">The results across the three evaluated datasets highlight the robustness and generalization capability of NeuroFusionNet. Performance remained consistently high in both eyes-closed and eyes-open conditions, indicating resilience to physiological variability. The model achieved balanced accuracy on the independent OSF dataset, despite differences in recording duration, demographics, and acquisition protocols. This demonstrates that NeuroFusionNet is not overfitted to a single dataset, but rather capable of adapting to heterogeneous EEG data distributions. Such cross-dataset stability is critical for clinical translation, where models must generalize beyond controlled research datasets to diverse real-world populations. Figure <a href="#Fig14" class="usa-link">14</a> provides a visual comparison of NeuroFusionNet’s performance across the three evaluated datasets. The model achieved the highest accuracy on the OpenNeuro eyes-closed dataset (94.27%), with only a marginal decrease under the eyes-open condition (92.15%). On the independent OSF dataset, performance remained strong (89.5% accuracy, 89.3% macro F1), despite differences in demographics, sampling rate, and recording duration. This consistency across datasets underscores the robustness of the proposed framework and its ability to generalize beyond a single recording protocol, reinforcing its potential for clinical deployment in heterogeneous populations.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig14"><h3 class="obj_head">Fig. 14.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/7914/12706009/625d261ccb22/41598_2025_28070_Fig14_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO14" height="395" width="549" alt="Fig. 14"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig14/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Cross-dataset validation results of NeuroFusionNet across ds004504 (eyes-closed), ds006036 (eyes-open), and OSF datasets with mean±SD.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par59">To provide formal cross-dataset validation evidence, we conducted an external evaluation protocol where the model trained on the OpenNeuro eyes-closed dataset (ds004504) was directly tested on the OSF dataset without retraining. As shown in Table <a href="#Tab25" class="usa-link">25</a>, the model achieved 88.4% accuracy and 88.0% macro F1 under this zero-shot transfer setting. This result confirms that NeuroFusionNet retains strong discriminative capability even when applied to unseen data distributions, supporting its potential for real-world clinical deployment.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/Tab25/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p id="Par60">To enhance the interpretability of the proposed model, we employed Grad-CAM-based visualization in two complementary representations. As shown in Fig. <a href="#Fig15" class="usa-link">15</a>, the first approach maps class-specific attention across EEG channels over time for AD, FTD, and CN groups. The model emphasizes the frontal and temporal regions in AD samples, consistent with established neurophysiological degeneration patterns. In contrast, FTD exhibits broader frontal activity, while CN demonstrates a more uniform distribution of attention across channels. Figure <a href="#Fig16" class="usa-link">16</a> projects the Grad-CAM activations onto a 2D scalp topography based on the standard 10–20 electrode placement. This spatial projection facilitates a clearer mapping between model-derived features and anatomically relevant brain regions, such as F3, T3, and Pz, thereby improving clinical insight. For a quantitative summary of cortical region band contributions derived from Grad-CAM and SHAP analyses, see Table <a href="#Tab28" class="usa-link">28</a>.</p>
